Johnson, now the world indoor record holder at 6.44, was clearly in
drama before the start of the final. First, a twitch by McRae set the field
off. Then Johnson got a brilliant and apparently valid start, only for the recall gun to be fired when he was halfway up the track.
While the 20,023 crowd booed, Johnson was penalised for a false
valid start, the muscular Canadian was again away blindingly fast. He
so quickly after the finish that he propelled himself into a somersault
over the padded barrier on the edge of the running oval. Johnson was
have run 6.36 if he had not been recalled from that second start. unhurt but was not completely happy, either, estimating that he would
In June 1989, Johnson admitted for the first time that he had taken
the 1988 Olympic Games. The World Athletics Council subsequently
stripped him of his world records and 1987 world title. McRae was thus
the official world record.

Olympic Games, Greece provided the most surprising winner of the
Paris World Indoor Championships. Papadiás showed he was a big
threat by recovering from a false start to win his semi-final in 6.53. In
the final most eyes were on Surin to see if the Canadian could take a
third title. In fact he could not even get among the medals
for most of the race. However, the Greek finished faster than anyone Green (6.49 in 1997) started best with Papadiás no better than fifth

Birmingham 2003
Final (Mar 14)
1, 2, Justin Gatlin USA 6.46 0.138
Kim Collins SKN 6.53 0.109
3, 4, Jason Gardener GBR 6.55 0.132
5, Gábor Dobos Mark Lewis-Francis GBR HUN 6.57 6.64 0.127 0.143
6, Jérôme Eyana FRA 6.64 0.141
7, 8, Markus Pöyhönen Deji Aliu FIN NGR 6.72 6.65 0.144 0.152
Gatlin had been lauded as a great talent in the United States but had
never raced outside of that country until the first round during the open-
ing session in Birmingham. He quickly made an impact, staying unusu-
ally low with his head down for half of the race before striding smooth-
ly away to win. Gatlin's compatriot Trammell went out in the first
round with an injured right hamstring. The semi-finals were won by
Collins of St Kitts appeared to struggle to reach the final but once there.
Collins edged Gardener, who repeated his 1999 bronze.

Moscow 2006
Final (Mar 10)
1, Leonard Scott USA 6.50 0.124
2, Andrey Yepishin RUS 6.52 0.144
3, Terrence Trammell USA 6.54 0.171
4, Matic Osovnikar SLO 6.58 0.155
5, Olusoji Fasuba NGR 6.58 0.163

Scott was the fastest entrant (6.52), and further enhanced his position
winners were local man Yepishin and hurdler Trammell, who had been
fastest in the heats with 6.60. Scott led from the gun in the final, but
was pressured all the way by the surprising Yepishin. Trammell, recov-
ering trom a stomach intection, was a close third.
"I never dreamt that a silver medal was possible," said Yepishin,
at 1500m in 1997.
The 2004 champion Jason Gardener vacated the title, preferring to
contest the Commonwealth Games (outdoors) in Melbourne.

1, 2, Dwain Chambers Mike Rodgers GBR USA 6.48 6.53 0.135 0.141
З, Daniel Bailey ANT 6.57 0.141

(5,) Samuel Francis QAT DQ (ADr10.8) (6.62) 0.149
Rightful finalist: Rodney Green BAH
fastest in the heats and semi-finals. Rodgers had the quickest reaction
in the final, then Chambers powered away to win by a half-metre from
the American. In fifth place, Nigerian transferee Francis provided the
Olympics showed a prohibited substance. Two years' worth of his
results were deleted.
For Chambers it was a first global gold medal, and the finest
moment in his controversial career which included a three-year doping
suspension. At 31 years 342 days, he became the oldest man to win this
particular title. A less welcome record was set by Switzerland's Fongué
in heat five of the first round. He became the first athlete at any World
Athletics Championship to be instantly disqualified under the tightened
false start ruling.

Former champion Gatlin got the better start, and powered away to
win the final by a metre from the Jamaican, with defending champion
Chambers just edging Kimmons for the bronze. For Gatlin it was a
repeat of his win from 2003, and in exactly the same time. "It feels like
a rebirth," remarked the returning drug offender.

There was no stopping Coleman, who'd twice bettered Maurice
Greene's 20-year old world record of 6.39 with marks of 6.37 and 6.34
fastest ever at sea level.
In the final, Coleman, Su and Baker had separated from the pack by
Biomechanical analysis of the start of the race showed, unsurprisingly, that Coleman had much the quickest "block time" of the finalists, that is
the total time spent in the block phase from initial movement to exit. His
was 0.441, compared with Su's 0.515. In fourth place again was Xie.
Collins, who won a silver at the last Birmingham edition in 2003,
broke his own record for the oldest ever male competitor. At 41 years
332 days he progressed to the semi-finals, but then scratched.

The East German teenager Schönlebe produced the best performance
of the 1985 Games by substantially improving the world best of 45.79.
Drawn in lane four, the reigning European Junior Champion raced
through 200m in 21.48 to lead by five metres. Bennett also ran hard
from lane 2 to hold second spot and gradually started to gain on the
"Actually, I got excited and went out too fast," admitted
Schönlebe. "But I was able to hold on, to my surprise - and pleasure."

with another terrific performance in Sopot, but it all came to nothing
Five men attempted 2.34. Three of those made it first time,
failed once then tried for 2.36, at which only the Ukrainian succeeded.

joined him, but failed with his first attempt. So did everyone else, until
the Russian guaranteed silver with his third try. At 2.40, both he and
Five men at 2.32 or better would have been the best depth since
1987, but the total became four after the disqualification of Ukhov. In
February 2019, he was one of 12 Russians sanctioned by the Court of
Arbitration For Sport for historic anti-doping rule violations. He'd been
failed (in 2021), all his performances between July 16, 2012 and
December 31, 2015 were annulled. Kynard replaced Ukhov as the 2012
Olympic Champion, and the American also got onto the Sopot podium,
once the Russian was stripped of his silver.

World Champion decathlete O'Brien got off to the best possible start
to this invitation event, literally, with a 0.104 reaction time in his 60m
score at the same stage in his world record performance. His four-event
score of 3800 still stands as a world best as at January 31, 2022
O'Brien continued way ahead of record pace on the second day,
scoring his fifth win out of five events in the 60m hurdles. A good pole

gained 100 points on the whole field.

out at 5.20 having only managed 5.00. Warner's 4.90 meant he was just

by five points. Another 0.43 would have meant gold for Warner.

The winning performance was ratified as a world record: faster
times by Shchennikov, Ronald Weigel and Frants Kostyukevich in
1988/89 were never ratified for various reasons.

A fantastic race which was not spoiled by the collisions and falls which

indoor relay. Poland, Jamaica and Britain followed

increased their lead on the third leg, thanks to Rysiukiewicz's split of
45.77. Meanwhile, Patrick was putting Britain into medal contention. He
clocked the fastest time on the third stage (45.39) to overtake Jamaica.

holding his blue baton by the tip, stole past at the finish to win for the
US in a world record of 3:02.83. The American's split of 44.75 remains
the fastest ever at the World Indoor Championships.

By halfway, the United States seemed to be heading for a runaway vic-
tory thanks to sub-46 legs by Campbell and Byrd. Their third man was
fielding the same quartet which had won silvers in 1999.
Young, the US anchor, could not shake off his pursuers.
Maćkowiak and Semyonov, both renowned relay runners, were close
behind on the final backstraight. The Russian was in third place and it
was he who kicked first, but Maćkowiak had the same idea and crept
up to Young's shoulder on the last bend then flashed past in the home-
straight. The Pole therefore made amends for 1999, when he had been
the one overtaken in the closing stages.
In 2003 it was confirmed that Young had committed a doping vio-
lation in June 1999 and all his performances until June 2001 - includ-
ing at Lisbon - were annulled. So Russia were promoted to silver with

AUS CAN 7.43
Rightful finalist: Rufina Ubah NGR DQ (7.08)
Canada had high hopes of duplicating Ben Johnson's 60m success in
the women's sprint, as Issajenko had recently lowered the world 50m
record. European Champion Cooman got the best start but appeared to
be caught by Issajenko just before the line. However, the official result
was announced as a narrow victory by the Dutchwoman.
Canada protested in vain over the result, and Cooman was regarded
as a lucky winner... until the Canadian's performance was later
annulled by the World Athletics Council. Issajenko, like Ben Johnson,
had admitted to drug-taking at the time of the championships.

The policy of allocating lanes of the 200m races in Paris was that the
three best qualifiers were given lanes 3, 4 or 5. Yet lane 6 proved to be
the lane in which both the men's and women's gold medallists were
the worst of the three "preferred" lanes, lane 3. The Jamaican raced into
speed. She could not respond when Koffa (lane 6) edged in front down
the finishing straight. As she placed second in her semi-final, Kóffa had
been given one of the unseeded lanes; she could just have easily been
placed in lane 1 or 2.
indoor 200m championships, the outermost lanes should go to the best
placed athletes in earlier rounds.

Seville 1991

Dixon felt she lost the 1989 title after being pushed at the bell. This
The American, wearing a special silver-grey headband, pulled away in
the second lap while Myers moved into second place. The American-
born Spaniard was poised to catch Dixon in the straight, but the New
Yorker did not fade and crossed the line in 50.64, the world's fastest
time in three years. It was the first world indoor championship title to
be won by a US woman - disregarding Dixon's own win at this event
in the 1985 Paris Games. All six finalists set personal bests, with the
first three setting national records.

would be between the two Russians.

The two halves of the race were covered in 4:26.6 & 4:12.2.

over, were treated to three progressively closer failures. Kostadinova therefore made it five world indoor wins out of five,
a unique achievement at the time and matched only by Cuban long
jumper Iván Pedroso in 2001.

Ennis, the 2009 World Heptathlon Champion, consolidated her posi-
producing a championship record of 4937, just ahead of Klüft's 4933
ers, including the long jump where she produced a lifetime best of 6.44.

In 2015, World Athletics announced that a re-test of Chernova's

The start of the final was delayed for 10 minutes after successive faulty
starts caused by problems with Jamaica's starting blocks, which were
eventually replaced. Initially, their lead-off runner Hall was warned
with a yellow card, but that was downgraded to a green when the tech-
nical difficulties became apparent.
Hastings put the United States into a clear lead which was
increased by individual finalists Atkins and McCorory. The latter ran a
relaxed first lap to enable Adeoye to get within range, but then took off
in the second half of her leg as Britain were caught by Jamaica at the
final changeover. Anchored by 400m hurdler Tate, the US won in
3:24.83, the quickest-ever non-Russian time.
Ohuruogu was no match for McPherson on the anchor and was in
danger of being caught by Russia, for whom Ryzhova produced a
fourth place, but was later disqualified for a doping violation.

The most golds won by an athlete is 22 (18 individual) by
most individual golds by a woman is 10 by Dibaba, Maria Mutola
(MOZ) and Valerie Adams/Vili (NZL). The most won by a country is
461 by the United States from Kenya 394 and Ethiopia 232. Next is
Russia on 167, or 202 if we add 28 Russian golds from the era of the USSR and the Unified Team, and seven "neutral" golds. The most
team-only golds, 121, have been won by the United States, ahead of Kenya (112) & Ethiopia (62).
Shown below are all gold-medal winning athletes and teams.
won golds in finals are included (in italics) as they too are awarded
medals. World Athletics also issued gold medals in cross country and
tinished the race (e.g. Tirunesh Dibaba in the short race at the 2006
World Cross), but this policy ended in June 2008. An athlete must
now finish legitimately to get a team medal, and will do so even if that
performance is non-scoring. Where there is the symbol X, the original
winning result has been adjusted due to disqualifications. Not counted
here are exhibition events or the 1985 World Indoor Games.
